Hyphal anastomosis is also used to categorize the isolates of R. solani into anastomosis groups (AG). Presently, 13 AGs have been described, several of which being divided References [SOURCE: Page_16]

into subgroups. Individual AGs are not strictly associated (2009) [SOURCE: Page_16]

(2005) [SOURCE: Page_16]

(2007) with a specific host but rather with a family of hosts which can be in turn narrow or very broad, for example AG 1 with rice mainly and AG 8 with various cereals. AG 3 isolates, and more specifically isolates from the AG 3 PT subgroup, are often Morphometric and molecular [SOURCE: Page_16]

Morphometric and molecular biochemical and molecular [SOURCE: Page_16]

associated with potato diseases (Fiers et al. in press; Kuninaga Morphometrics, host range, [SOURCE: Page_16]

(PCR-RFLP) analysis et al. 2000; Carling et al. 2002). However it was shown, in Great Britain and France, that AG 2–1 and AG 5 can cause Detection methods [SOURCE: Page_16]

(RFLP) analysis [SOURCE: Page_16]

disease in potato crops but with a much lower incidence than AG 3 PT (Campion et al. 2003; Woodhall et al. 2007). analysis [SOURCE: Page_16]

As a result of the genetic evolution of pathogens, new PCR [SOURCE: Page_16]

pathotypes are regularly discovered. Conversely, some populations such as P. erythroseptica and Clavibacter michiganensis subsp. sepedonicus vary slightly in pathoge- Main penetration [SOURCE: Page_16]

nicity and in genetic diversity suggesting a relatively recent Mechanical and enzymatic [SOURCE: Page_16]

introduction of a small founding population of the pathogen Mechanical [SOURCE: Page_16]

Enzymatic [SOURCE: Page_16]

(Smith et al. 2001; Peters et al. 2005). Genetic evolution ways [SOURCE: Page_16]

can be achieved by vertical or horizontal gene transfer. Meloidogyne populations originally did not possess the cell wall-degrading enzymes required to invade host roots. Although the mechanism of horizontal gene transfer Conservation and [SOURCE: Page_16]

remains largely elusive, it has been speculated that a gene overwintering [SOURCE: Page_16]

coding for a cell wall-degrading enzyme was horizontally transferred from a rhizobial bacterium to the nematode and was kept in the genome of the nematode by strong selection pressures representing important initial steps facilitating the invasion of plants by nematodes (Dieterich and Sommer arenaria, M. incognita, [SOURCE: Page_16]

and yield losses, even if the infestation level is low. Classical detection methods begin with visual observation Disease [SOURCE: Page_16]

The anti-microbial agents produced by potatoes can be glycoalkaloids (α-chaconin and α-solanine), phenolic compounds and phytoalexins, antimicrobial com- pounds produced by the plant after pathogen attacks (Okopnyi et al. 1983; Lyon 1989; Ray and Hammerschmidt 1998; Zagoskina et al. 2006; Baker et al. 2008; Lerat et al. 2009). 2006). Another plant defense reaction called systemic acquired resistance (SAR) spreads a signal through the surrounding V. dahliae, R. solani [SOURCE: Page_19]

cells. It allows plants to become highly resistant to subsequent infection by the original pathogen but also by a wide variety of other pathogens. For example, foliar SAR-inducing applica- tions of (benzo (1,2,3) thiadiazole-7-carbothioic acid S-methyl ester-BTH and harpin) reduce the numbers of root lesion nematodes (Pratylenchus spp.) and root knot nematodes (Meloidogyne chitwoodi; Collins et al. 2006). 4.5 Pesticides Storage [SOURCE: Page_24]

Pesticides are commonly used to control various pathogens altering potato tubers. They can be applied as soil fumigant Cultural systems [SOURCE: Page_24]

(fumigants such as carbamates are not allowed in some European countries), sprayed or powdered directly on seed Organic [SOURCE: Page_24]

tubers after harvest or applied as granular (Hide et al. 1995; Tsror et al. 2000; Errampalli et al. 2006). The chemicals have to be carefully chosen, since pathogens can adapt and become oxamyl, fosthiazate, 1,3-dichloropropene Aldicarb (+ oxamyl), [SOURCE: Page_24]

1,3-dichloropropene, [SOURCE: Page_24]

resistant (Table 8). Thiabendazole-resistance was detected in Fusarium avenaceum, F. culmorum, F. equiseti, and F. carbofuran cadusafos, Pesticides [SOURCE: Page_24]

sporotrichioides (Fusarium dry rot; Ocamb et al. 2007), in P. pustulans (skin spot; Carnegie et al. 2008) and in H. solani (silver scurf). Mefenoxam-resistance is known for P. eryth- roseptica (pink rot) populations (Taylor et al. 2006) and Planting, lifting, and harvesting methods [SOURCE: Page_24]

numerous treatments of carbendazim select resistant mutants of Sclerotium rolfsii (stem rot; Solunke et al. 2001). Moreover, the use of numerous chemicals is nowadays regulated and many of them are no longer permitted in Europe. [SOURCE: Page_24]

4.6 Organic farming versus conventional agriculture Tillage [SOURCE: Page_24]

Organic farming relies on agricultural techniques that exclude the use of chemical pesticides and recommend organic Fertiliaation and [SOURCE: Page_24]

fertilization. As a result, the soil and tuber environment is amendments [SOURCE: Page_24]

quite different from the one caused by conventional practices and may induce disease suppression (Table 8). To reduce disease incidence or severity, the best adapted cultural grasses Without [SOURCE: Page_24]

sundangrass or [SOURCE: Page_24]
